      <a name="sec3" class="anchor"></a>
      <hr class="docs">
      
      <h3>3. Important Additional Files</h3>
            
      <a name="sec3a" class="anchor"></a>

      <h4>Offsets</h4>

      <p>The "offsets" files are simply helpers which facilitate rapid loading of data. If you want to use the <a href="/data/docs/scripts/">helper scripts</a> for working with the actual data files (snapshots or group catalogs) on your local machine, then it <b>is required</b> that you download the offset file(s) for the snapshot(s) you are interested in working with.</p>

      <p>If you only work with the web-based tools and API (e.g. only analyze particle-level data using cutouts), then downloading offset files is <b>not required</b>.</p>

      <p><span class="newtng">Note that in Illustris, offsets were embedded inside the group catalog files for convenience. In TNG however, we have kept offsets as a separate HDF5 file (one per snapshot), which need to be downloaded as required.</span> Most simply, you can think of offsets as describing where in the group catalog files to find a specific halo/subhalo, and where in the snapshot files to find the start of the particles of a given halo/subhalo.</p>

      <p>The following table describes the fields in each offsets file.</p>

      <div style="text-align:center;">
        <table class="table table-bordered table-condensed eoTable">        
          <tr>
          <th> Field </th>
          <th> Dimensions </th>
          <th> Description </th>
          </tr>
          <tr><td> FileOffsets/SnapByType
          </td><td> [$6, N_{\rm c}$] int array
          </td><td> The offset table (by type) for the snapshot files, giving the first particle index in each snap file chunk. Determines which files(s) a given offset+length will cover. 
          A two-dimensional array, where the element $(i,j)$ equals the cumulative sum (i.e. offset) of particles of type $i$ in all snapshot file chunks prior to $j$.
          </tr><tr>
          </td><td> FileOffsets/Group
          </td><td> [$N_{\rm c}$] int array
          </td><td> The offset table for groups in the group catalog files. A one-dimensional array, where the $i^{th}$ element equals the first group number in the $i^{th}$ groupcat file chunk.
          </tr><tr>
          </td><td> FileOffsets/Subhalo
          </td><td> [$N_{\rm c}$] int array
          </td><td> The offset table for subhalos in the group catalog files. A one-dimensional array, where the $i^{th}$ element equals the first subgroup number in the $i^{th}$ groupcat file chunk.
          </tr><tr>
          </td><td> FileOffsets/SubLink
          </td><td> [$N_{\rm c}$] int array
          </td><td> The offset table for trees in the SubLink files. A one-dimensional array, where the $i^{th}$ element equals the first tree number in the $i^{th}$ SubLink file chunk.
          </td></tr>
          <tr>
          <td> Group/SnapByType
          </td><td> Ngroups_Total,6
          </td><td> The offset table for a given group number (by type), into the snapshot files. That is, the global particle index (across all snap file chunks) of the first particle of this group.
          A two-dimensional array, where the element $(i,j)$ equals the cumulative sum (i.e. offset) of particles of type $i$ in all groups prior to group number $j$.
          </tr><tr>
          <td> Subhalo/SnapByType
          </td><td> Nsubgroups_Total,6
          </td><td> The offset table for a given subhalo number (by type), into the snapshot files. That is, the global particle index (across all snap file chunks) of the first particle of this subhalo.
          A two-dimensional array, where the element $(i,j)$ equals the cumulative sum (i.e. offset) of particles of type $i$ in all subhalos prior to subhalo number $j$.
          </tr><tr>
          <td> Subhalo/LHaloTree/File
          </td><td> Nsubgroups_Total
          </td><td> The LHaloTree file number with the tree which contains this subhalo.
          </tr><tr>
          <td> Subhalo/LHaloTree/Num
          </td><td> Nsubgroups_Total
          </td><td> The number of the tree within the above file within which this subhalo is located (e.g. TreeX).
          </tr><tr>
          <td> Subhalo/LHaloTree/Index
          </td><td> Nsubgroups_Total
          </td><td> The LHaloTree index within the above tree dataset at which this subhalo is located.
          </tr><tr>
          <td> Subhalo/Sublink/RowNum
          </td><td> Nsubgroups_Total
          </td><td> The SubLink global index of the location of this subhalo.
          </tr><tr>
          <td> Subhalo/Sublink/SubhaloID
          </td><td> Nsubgroups_Total
          </td><td> The Sublink ID of this subhalo.
          </tr><tr>
          <td> Subhalo/Sublink/LastProgenitorID
          </td><td> Nsubgroups_Total
          </td><td> The SubLink ID of the last progenitor of this tree (all the subhalos contained in the tree rooted in this subhalo are the ones with IDs between SubhaloID and LastProgenitorID).
          </td>
          </tr>
        </table>
      </div>
      
      <a name="sec3b" class="anchor"></a>

      <h4>The "simulation.hdf5" File</h4>

      <p>Each run has a single file available called <span class="newtng"><code>simulation.hdf5</code></span> which is <b>purely optional, for convenience, and not required by any of the helper scripts or examples</b>. Its purpose is to encapsulate <i>all</i> data of an <i>entire</i> simulation into a single file. They can be downloaded on each simulation page.</p>

      <p>To accomplish this, we make advantage of a new feature of the HDF5 library called "virtual datasets". You can think of a virtual dataset as a collection of symbolic links to one or more datasets in other HDF5 file(s), where these symlinks can refer to subsets of a dataset, in either the source or target of the link. The simulation.hdf5 is thus a large collection of "links", which refer to other files which actually contain data. In order to use it, you must therefore also download the necessary files (e.g. of snapshots, group catalogs, or supplementary data catalogs).</p>

      <p>What does it let us do? First of all, no more file chunks! The fact that a snapshot or group catalog is split over multiple files is no longer relevant. Loading becomes very simple:</p>

<div class="collapse in lang_python"><div class="highlight"><pre><span></span>with h5py.File<span class="o">(</span><span class="s1">&#39;simulation.hdf5&#39;</span>,<span class="s1">&#39;r&#39;</span><span class="o">)</span> as f:
    <span class="nv">gas_cell_mass</span> <span class="o">=</span> f<span class="o">[</span><span class="s1">&#39;/Snapshots/99/PartType0/Masses&#39;</span><span class="o">][()]</span>
    <span class="nv">subhalo_size_stars</span> <span class="o">=</span> f<span class="o">[</span><span class="s1">&#39;/Groups/99/Subhalos/SubhaloHalfmassRadType&#39;</span><span class="o">][</span>:,4<span class="o">]</span>
</pre></div>
</div>

      <p>where <code>gas_cell_mass</code> then contains the masses of every gas cell in the entire z=0 snapshot, while <code>subhalo_size_stars</code> contains the stellar half mass radii of all the subhalos in the z=0 group catalog. The simulation.hdf5 also makes loading the particles of a given halo or subhalo trivial:</p>

<div class="collapse in lang_python"><div class="highlight"><pre><span></span><span class="nv">halo_id</span> <span class="o">=</span> <span class="m">400</span>
<span class="nv">part_type</span> <span class="o">=</span> <span class="m">1</span>

with h5py.File<span class="o">(</span><span class="s1">&#39;simulation.hdf5&#39;</span>,<span class="s1">&#39;r&#39;</span><span class="o">)</span> as f:
    <span class="nv">start</span> <span class="o">=</span> f<span class="o">[</span><span class="s1">&#39;/Offsets/99/Group/SnapByType&#39;</span><span class="o">][</span>halo_id, part_type<span class="o">]</span>
    <span class="nv">length</span> <span class="o">=</span> f<span class="o">[</span><span class="s1">&#39;/Groups/99/Group/GroupLenType&#39;</span><span class="o">][</span>halo_id, part_type<span class="o">]</span>

    <span class="nv">dm_positions</span> <span class="o">=</span> f<span class="o">[</span><span class="s1">&#39;/Snapshots/99/PartType1/Coordinates&#39;</span><span class="o">][</span>start:start+length,:<span class="o">]</span>
</pre></div>
</div>

      <p>where <code>dm_positions</code> would then contain the dark matter coordinates of every DM particle belonging to halo index 400 of the given simulation.</p>

      <p>Finally, supplementary data catalogs (either those we provide, or similar computations you have run yourself) can be <i>virtually</i> inserted at any time as datasets in snapshots or group catalogs. This provides a nice, clean way to organize post-processing computations which result in value-added values for halos, subhalos, or individual particles/cells. You can then load such catalogs with the same scripts (and same syntax) as 'original' snapshot/group catalog fields.</p>

      <p>There are two requirements to use a simulation.hdf5 file:</p>

      <ul>
        <li> Actual data files must be organized exactly as suggested and described in the <a href="/data/docs/scripts/">example scripts tutorial</a> (i.e., an "output" directory containing a "snapdir_099" subdirectory and a "group_099" subdirectory, along with a "postprocessing" directory containing, among others, an "offsets" subdirectory). </li>

        <li> HDF5 <a href="http://docs.h5py.org/en/latest/vds.html">virtual datasets</a> are a new feature, only supported by HDF5 version 1.10.x and later. This is not presently the default version installed on most clusters, which still use 1.8.x. You may have to install a newer version yourself, or request that your system administrator do so. Note! Files created with advanced features of the new HDF5 library series are not backwards compatible. The old 1.8.x series of HDF5 reaches end of life in 2019, so it is a good idea regardless to migrate. In Python, you will also need a fairly new version of h5py (e.g. 2.9.x). </li>
      </ul>
      
      <a name="sec4" class="anchor"></a>
      <hr class="docs">
      
      <h3>4. Merger Trees</h3>
      
      <p>Merger trees have been created for the various Illustris simulations using SubLink 
      <a href="http://adsabs.harvard.edu/cgi-bin/bib_query?arXiv:1502.01339">(Rodriguez-Gomez+ 2015)</a> 
      and LHaloTree <a href="http://adsabs.harvard.edu/abs/2005Natur.435..629S">(Springel+ 2005)</a>.
      The LHaloTree are essentially identical to the primary trees of the Millennium and Aquarius simulations, 
      but in HDF5 format. 
      <span class="sametng">Merger tree formats in TNG are identical to Illustris, with additional fields from the group catalogs also present.</span> 
      In the population average sense the different merger trees give similar results. In more 
      detail, the exact merger history or mass assembly history for any given halo may differ. For any given science 
      goal, one type of tree may be more or less useful, and users are free to use whichever they prefer. These codes 
      are all included in the Sussing Merger Trees comparison project 
      <a href="http://adsabs.harvard.edu/cgi-bin/bib_query?arXiv:1307.3577">(Srisawat+ 2013)</a>.</p>
      
      <p>The following figure shows a schematic of the structure of both the SubLink and LHaloTree merger trees. It is 
      not necessary to understand the complete details of the trees to practically use them. In particular, the only 
      critical links are the 'descendant' (black), 'first progenitor' (green), and 'next progenitor' (red) associations. 
      These are shown for all tree nodes in the diagram. For their exact definitions, see the tables below. Walking 
      back in time following along the main (most massive) progenitor branch consists of following the first progenitor 
      links until they end (value equals -1). Similarly, walking forward in time along the descendants branch consists 
      of following the descendant links until they end (value equals -1), which typically occurs at $z=0$. The full 
      progenitor history, and not just the main branch, requires following both the first and next progenitor links. 
      In this way the user can identify all subhalos at a previous snapshot which have a common descendant. Examples 
      of walking the tree are provided in the example scripts.</p>
      
      <img class="img-responsive" src="/static/data/specifications_mergertree.png">
      
      <p style="text-align:center;margin: -10px auto 30px auto; font-size:80%;"><b>Caption.</b> Schematic diagram of the 
      merger tree structure for both SubLink and LHaloTree. Both algorithms connect subhalos across different snapshots 
      in the simulation. Rows indicate discrete snapshots, with time increasing downwards towards redshift zero 
      (the horizontal axis is arbitrary). Green circles represent subhalos (the nodes of the merger tree), while beige 
      boxes indicate the grouping of the subhalos into their parent FoF groups. The most important links are for the 
      descendant (black), first progenitor (green), and next progenitor (red), which are shown for all subhalos. The 
      root descendant (purple), last progenitor (blue), and main leaf progenitor (orange) links exist only for the 
      SubLink trees, and for simplicity these last three link types are shown only for subhalos 5, 7, and 19 
      (darker striped circles).</p>
      
      <p>The number inside each circle from the figure is the unique ID (within the whole simulation) of the 
      corresponding subhalo, which is assigned in a depth-first fashion. Numbering also indicates the on-disk storage 
      ordering for the SubLink trees, which adopt the approach of 
      <a href="http://adsabs.harvard.edu/abs/2006ASPC..351..212L">Lemson+ (2006)</a>. For example, the main progenitor 
      branch (from 5-7 in the example) and the full progenitor tree (from 5-13 in the example) are both contiguous 
      subsets of each merger tree field, whose location and size can be calculated using these links. The ordering 
      within a single tree in the LHaloTree is not guaranteed to follow this scheme.</p>
      
      <p>The 'root descendant' (purple), 'last progenitor' (blue), and 'main leaf progenitor' (orange) links exist only 
      for the SubLink trees. For simplicity, these last three link types are shown only for nodes 5, 7, and 19 (darker 
      striped circles). Using these links is optional, but allows efficient extraction of main progenitor branches, 
      subtrees (i.e., the set containing a subhalo and "all" its progenitors), "forward" descendant branches, and other 
      subsets of the tree. For their full definitions, see the following table.</p>
      
      <p>Each subhalo spans a "subtree" consisting of the subhalo itself and all its progenitors. As an example, the 
      subhalos belonging to the subtree of subhalo 5 are shown in darker green in the figure. Other subhalos not 
      belonging to this subtree are shown in lighter green, and their links are indicated with dashed arrows. In the 
      SubLink trees, the subtree of any subhalo can be extracted easily using the 'last progenitor' pointer. As shown 
      in the figure, since subhalo 13 is the 'last progenitor' of subhalo 5, the subtree of subhalo 5 consists of all 
      subhalos with IDs between 5 and 13. Similarly, the main progenitor branch of any subhalo can be retrieved 
      efficiently using the 'main leaf progenitor' link.</p>
      
      <p>Both SubLink and LHaloTree contain the links 'first subhalo in FoF group' (light brown dotted arrow) and 
      'next subhalo in FoF group' (dark brown dotted arrow), which connect subhalos that belong to the same FoF group. 
      The FoF groups do not play a direct role in the construction of the merger tree. However, in SubLink, subhalos 
      that belong to the same FoF group are also considered to be part of the same tree. As a result, two otherwise 
      independent trees (based on the progenitor and descendant links) are considered to be the same tree if they are 
      "connected" by a FoF group. This is exemplified in the figure by the FoF group containing subhalos 12, 16, and 20. 
      This FoF group acts as a bridge between the left and right trees.</p>
      
      <a name="sec4a" class="anchor"></a>

      <h4>SubLink</h4>
      
      <p>The SubLink algorithm constructs merger trees at the subhalo level. A unique descendant is assigned to each 
      subhalo in three steps (see <a href="http://adsabs.harvard.edu/cgi-bin/bib_query?arXiv:1502.01339">Rodriguez-Gomez+ 
      2015</a>). First, descendant candidates are identified for each subhalo as those subhalos in the following snapshot 
      that have common particles with the subhalo in question. Second, each of the descendant candidates is given a 
      score based on a merit function that takes into account the binding energy rank of each particle. Third, the 
      unique descendant of the subhalo in question is the descendant candidate with the highest score. Sometimes the 
      halo finder does not detect a small subhalo that is passing through a larger structure, because the density 
      contrast is not high enough. {\sc SubLink} deals with this issue by allowing some subhalos to skip a snapshot 
      when finding a descendant. Once all descendant connections have been made, the main progenitor of each subhalo 
      is defined as the one with the "most massive history" behind it.</p>
     
      <p>Note that this merger tree comes in two varieties: "dark-matter based" and "baryonic based". The dark-matter 
      based tree is the fiducial choice, and has been publicly released as "SubLink". The baryonic based tree is 
      called "SubLink_gal" and has not been publicly released for simplicity, although it is available upon request.
      While the two will largely give identical results, depending on scientific application, one might be more 
      useful than the other.</p>
 
      <p>The SubLink merger tree is one large data structure split across several sequential HDF5 files named 
      <code>tree_extended.[fileNum].hdf5</code>, where <code>[fileNum]</code> goes from e.g. 0 to 9 for the Illustris-1 run. These 
      files store the data on a per tree basis, and therefore are completely independent from each other. More 
      specifically, any two subhalos that are connected by any of the pointers described in the SubLink table are 
      guaranteed to belong to the same tree, and, therefore, their data is found in the same file. The following 
      table lists the fields which are present in each file.</p>
      
      <div style="text-align:center;">
        <table class="table table-bordered table-condensed eoTable">
          <tr>
          <th> Field </th>
          <th> DataType </th>
          <th> Dimensions </th>
          <th> Units </th>
          <th> Description </th>
          </tr>
          <tr>
          <td> SubhaloID
          </td><td> int64
          </td><td> (N)
          </td><td> -
          </td><td> Unique identifier of this subhalo, assigned in a "depth-first" fashion (Lemson & Springel 2006). This value is contiguous within a single tree.
          </tr><tr>
          </td><td> SubhaloIDRaw
          </td><td> int64
          </td><td> (N)
          </td><td> -
          </td><td> Unique identifier of this subhalo in raw format (= SnapNum*10^12 + SubfindID).
          </tr><tr>
          </td><td> LastProgenitorID 
          </td><td> int64
          </td><td> (N)
          </td><td> -
          </td><td> The SubhaloID of the last progenitor of the tree rooted at this subhalo. Since the SubhaloIDs are assigned in a "depth-first" fashion, all the subhalos contained in the tree rooted at this subhalo are the ones with SubhaloIDs between (and including) the SubhaloID and LastProgenitorID of this subhalo. For subhalos with no progenitors, LastProgenitorID == SubhaloID.
          </tr><tr>
          </td><td> MainLeafProgenitorID 
          </td><td> int64 
          </td><td> (N)
          </td><td> -
          </td><td> The SubhaloID of the last progenitor along the main branch, i.e. the earliest progenitor obtained by following the FirstProgenitorID pointer. For subhalos with no progenitors, MainLeafProgenitorID == SubhaloID.
          </tr><tr>
          </td><td> RootDescendantID 
          </td><td> int64 
          </td><td> (N)
          </td><td> -
          </td><td> The SubhaloID of the latest subhalo that can be reached by following the DescendantID link, i.e. the root of the tree to which this subhalo belongs. For subhalos with no descendants, RootDescendantID == SubhaloID.
          </tr><tr>
          </td><td> TreeID 
          </td><td> int64 
          </td><td> (N)
          </td><td> -
          </td><td> Unique identifier of the tree to which this subhalo belongs.
          </tr><tr>
          </td><td> SnapNum 
          </td><td> int16 
          </td><td> (N)
          </td><td> -
          </td><td> The snapshot in which this subhalo is found.
          </tr><tr>
          </td><td> FirstProgenitorID 
          </td><td> int64 
          </td><td> (N)
          </td><td> -
          </td><td> The SubhaloID of this subhalo's first progenitor. The first progenitor is the one with the "most massive history" behind it (following De Lucia & Blaizot 2007). For subhalos with no progenitors, FirstProgenitorID == -1.
          </tr><tr>
          </td><td> NextProgenitorID 
          </td><td> int64 
          </td><td> (N)
          </td><td> -
          </td><td> The SubhaloID of the subhalo with the next most massive history which shares the same descendant as this subhalo. If there are no more subhalos sharing the same descendant, NextProgenitorID == -1.
          </tr><tr>
          </td><td> DescendantID 
          </td><td> int64 
          </td><td> (N)
          </td><td> -
          </td><td> The SubhaloID of this subhalo's descendant. If this subhalo has no descendants, DescendantID == -1.
          </tr><tr>
          </td><td> FirstSubhaloInFOFGroupID 
          </td><td> int64 
          </td><td> (N)
          </td><td> -
          </td><td> The SubhaloID of the first subhalo (i.e., the one with the most massive history) from the same FOF group.
          </tr><tr>
          </td><td> NextSubhaloInFOFGroupID 
          </td><td> int64 
          </td><td> (N)
          </td><td> -
          </td><td> The SubhaloID of the next subhalo (ordered by their mass history) from the same FOF group. If there are no more subhalos in the same FOF group, NextSubhaloInFOFGroupID == -1.
          </tr><tr>
          </td><td> NumParticles 
          </td><td> uint32 
          </td><td> (N)
          </td><td> -
          </td><td> Number of particles in the current subhalo which were used in the merger tree to determine descendants (e.g. DM-only or stars + star-forming gas).
          </tr><tr>
          </td><td> Mass 
          </td><td> float32 
          </td><td> (N)
          </td><td> $ 10^{10} M_\odot/h $
          </td><td> Mass of the current subhalo, including only the particles which were used in the merger tree to determine descendants (e.g. DM-only or stars + star-forming gas).
          </tr><tr>
          </td><td> MassHistory 
          </td><td> float32 
          </td><td> (N)
          </td><td> $ 10^{10} M_\odot/h $
          </td><td> Sum of the Mass field of all progenitors along the main branch (De Lucia &amp; Blaizot 2007).
          </tr><tr>
          </td><td> SubfindID 
          </td><td> int32 
          </td><td> (N)
          </td><td> -
          </td><td> Index of this subhalo in the Subfind group catalog.
          </tr><tr>
          <td colspan="5">
          All original fields from the group catalogs are also available, reordered into the same order 
          as the merger trees for convenience.
          See the group catalog description for their units, dimensions, and descriptions.

          <br><b>Fields:</b> 
          Group_M_Crit200, 
          Group_M_Mean200, 
          Group_M_TopHat200, 
          SubhaloBHMass, 
          SubhaloBHMdot,
          SubhaloCM,
          ...

          <br><b>Note:</b> Group_M_Crit200, Group_M_Mean200, and Group_M_Tophat200 are FOF group quantities, so that all subhalos from the same FOF group will have the same value.
          </td>
          </tr>
        </table>
      </div>
      
      <a name="sec4b" class="anchor"></a>

      <h4>LHaloTree</h4>
      
      <p>The LHaloTree algorithm is virtually identical to that used for the Millennium simulation, 
      constructing trees based on subhalos instead of main halos, described fully in the supplementary information of 
      <a href="http://adsabs.harvard.edu/abs/2005Natur.435..629S">Springel+ (2005)</a>. In short, to determine the 
      appropriate descendant, the unique IDs that label each particle are tracked between outputs. For a given halo, 
      the algorithm finds all halos in the subsequent output that contain some of its particles. These are then counted 
      in a weighted fashion, giving higher weight to particles that are more tightly bound in the halo under 
      consideration, and the one with the highest count is selected as the descendant. In this way, preference is given 
      to tracking the fate of the inner parts of a structure, which may survive for a long time upon infall into a 
      bigger halo, even though much of the mass in the outer parts can be quickly stripped. To allow for the possibility 
      that halos may temporarily disappear for one snapshot, the process is repeated for Snapshot n to Snapshot 
      n+2. If either there is a descendant found in Snapshot $n + 2$ but none found in Snapshot n+1, or, if the 
      descendant in Snapshot n+1 has several direct progenitors and the descendant in Snapshot n+2 has only one, 
      then a link is made that skips the intervening snapshot.</p>

      <p>The LHaloTree merger tree is one large data structure split across several HDF5 files named 
      <code>trees_sf1_<span class="newtng">99</span>.[chunkNum].hdf5</code>, where [chunkNum] goes from e.g. 0 to 511 for the Illustris-1 run.
      Within each file there are a number of groups named <code>TreeX</code>, where <code>X</code> is an integer 
      which simply increases from zero to the number of tree groups in that file chunk. Note that a given TreeX 
      group may contain subhalos spanning different FoF groups as well as snapshots, and to efficiently locate a 
      specific subhalo at a specific snapshot (e.g z=0) the offsets can be used. The pair 
      <code>(SubhaloNumber,SnapNum)</code> provides the indexing into the Subfind group 
      catalog. The five other indices for each entry in a TreeX group (e.g. <code>Descendant</code>) index into 
      that same group in the tree file. 
      The following tables describe the fields. First, the Header group:</p>

      <div style="text-align:center;">
        <table class="table table-bordered table-condensed eoTable">
          <tr>
          <th> Dataset </th>
          <th> Dimensions </th>
          <th> Units </th>
          <th> Description </th>
          </tr>
          <tr>
          <td> Redshifts
          </td><td> {N_snap}
          </td><td> -
          </td><td> List of redshifts of the snapshots used to create this merger tree.
          </tr><tr>
          </td><td> TotNsubhalos
          </td><td> {N_snap}
          </td><td> -
          </td><td> Equal to the number of Subfind/Subhalo groups in the group catalog, for each snapshot used to create this merger tree.
          </tr><tr>
          </td><td> TreeNHalos
          </td><td> {N_halos}
          </td><td> -
          </td><td> 'The size of {N} for each "TreeX" group in this file', e.g. the total number of halos (across time) in that group.
          </tr><tr>
          </td><td> FirstSnapshotNr
          </td><td> 1
          </td><td> -
          </td><td> First snapshot number used to make these merger trees (should be 0).
          </tr><tr>
          </td><td> LastSnapshotNr
          </td><td> 1
          </td><td> -
          </td><td> Last snapshot number used to make these merger trees (should be 99 for TNG).
          </tr><tr>
          </td><td> SnapSkipFac
          </td><td> 1
          </td><td> -
          </td><td> Snapshot stride when making these merger trees (should be 1).
          </tr><tr>
          </td><td> NtreesPerFile
          </td><td> 1
          </td><td> -
          </td><td> 'The size of {N_halos} for this file', can be used to calculate the offset to map a FoF group number to a "TreeX" group name (made to be roughly equal across chunks).
          </tr><tr>
          </td><td> NhalosPerFile
          </td><td> 1
          </td><td> -
          </td><td> The total number of tree members (subhalos) 'in this file.' Equals the sum of all elements of TreeNHalos.
          </tr><tr>
          </td><td> ParticleMass
          </td><td> 1
          </td><td> $ 10^{10} M_\odot / h $
          </td><td> The dark matter particle mass used to make these merger trees.
          </td>
          </tr>
        </table>
      </div>
      
      <p>TreeX Groups:</p>
      
      <div style="text-align:center;">
        <table class="table table-bordered table-condensed eoTable">
          <tr>
          <th> Dataset </th>
          <th> Dimensions </th>
          <th> Description </th>
          </tr>
          <tr>
          <td> SubhaloNumber 
          </td><td> (N) 
          </td><td> The ID of this subhalo, unique within the full simulation for this snapshot. Indexes the Subfind group catalog at SnapNum.
          </tr><tr>
          </td><td> Descendant 
          </td><td> (N) 
          </td><td> The index of the subhalo's descendant within the merger tree, if any (-1 otherwise). Indexes this TreeX group.
          </tr><tr>
          </td><td> FirstProgenitor 
          </td><td> (N) 
          </td><td> The index of the subhalo's first progenitor within the merger tree, if any (-1 otherwise). The first progenitor is defined as the most massive one (-1 if none). Indexes this TreeX group.
          </tr><tr>
          </td><td> NextProgenitor 
          </td><td> (N) 
          </td><td> The index of the next subhalo from the same snapshot which shares the same descendant, if any (-1 if this is the last). Indexes this TreeX group.
          </tr><tr>
          </td><td> FirstHaloInFOFGroup 
          </td><td> (N) 
          </td><td> The index of the main subhalo (i.e. the most massive one) from the same FOF group.  Indexes this TreeX group.
          </tr><tr>
          </td><td> NextHaloInFOFGroup 
          </td><td> (N) 
          </td><td> The index of the next subhalo from the same FOF group (-1 if this is the last). Indexes this TreeX group.
          </tr><tr>
          </td><td> FileNr 
          </td><td> (N) 
          </td><td> File number in which the subhalo is found. (Redundant, i.e. for a given [chunkNum] file, this array will be constant and equal to [chunkNum])
          </tr><tr>
          </td><td> SnapNum 
          </td><td> (N) 
          </td><td> The snapshot in which this subhalo was found. 
          </tr><tr>
          <td colspan="3">
          Most all original fields from the group catalogs are also available, reordered into the same order 
          as the merger trees for convenience.
          See the group catalog description for their units, dimensions, and descriptions.

          <br><b>Fields:</b> 
          Group_M_Crit200, 
          Group_M_Mean200, 
          Group_M_TopHat200, 
          SubhaloBHMass, 
          SubhaloBHMdot,
          SubhaloCM,
          ...

          <br><b>Note:</b> Group_M_Crit200, Group_M_Mean200, and Group_M_Tophat200 are FOF group quantities, so that only the first subgroup from each FOF group will have a nonzero value.
          </td>
          </tr>
        </table>
      </div>

      
      
      <a name="sec5" class="anchor"></a>
      <hr class="docs">
      
      <h3>5. Supplementary Data Catalogs</h3>

      <p>"Supplementary" or "post-processing" catalogs have been created by various members of the TNG team, extended collaboration, and general public. They typically contain valuable (and often computationally expensive) calculations, which are released here in the hope that they will accelerate the science and productivity of all users of the TNG data. Most often, they are catalogs of physical quantities calculated for halos or subhalos, at one or more snapshots. Each will have a somewhat different structure, so one should carefully read the provided documentation. To use any of these catalogs, one should download the respective HDF5 file(s) and directly load them using e.g. <code>h5py</code>.</p>

      <a name="sec5a" class="anchor"></a>
      <hr class="docs">
      <h3 class="docs">(a) Tracer Tracks</h3>

      

<p>The <a href="/data/docs/specifications/#parttype3">Monte Carlo tracer particles</a> can be difficult to 
  work with, and this dataset aims to enable some simple 
  use cases. In particular, a number of catalogs are available, each of which contains information on all the 
  tracers in the simulation which are contained within (all) FoF halos at redshift zero. Tracers have been 
  re-arranged into "group order", such that you can quickly load the tracers which belong to a given subhalo or 
  halo. Further, their evolution through time has been saved, and these "tracks" through time are immediately 
  available, such that one does not need to load previous snapshots.</p>

<p>A series of files with the common base "tr_all_groups_99_*" indicating that each contains information on all 
   the tracers in the simulation which are contained within FoF halos at snapshot 99 (z=0). The 'meta' file 
   contains the IDs of the tracers (and their parents), which are ordered according to their halo/subhalo membership, 
   exactly following the group-ordering of the particles in the snapshots. Therefore, the order of the ParentIDs 
   list is the same as those IDs appear in the snapshots (although with possible omissions or duplications if a 
   parent has no, or multiple, tracers). The 'meta' file also contains the lengths and offsets, for both halos and 
   subhalos (in exact analogy to GroupLenType, SubhaloLenType, offsets/Group/SnapByType, and offsets/Subhalo/SnapByType). 
  This allows the child tracers of any halo or subhalo (at z=0) to be selected.</p>

<p>Note that tracers are stored separately by parent type (at z=0), either gas, stars (pt4), or bhs.</p>

<p>All other 'data' files (e.g. temp, pos, ...) contain the evolutionary tracks of this property, for the identical 
   set of tracers through time, recorded for all snapshots which contain tracers. For TNG100 these are the 20 full 
   snapshots, while for TNG50 and TNG300 these are all 100 snapshots.</p>

<p>For example, the temperature history of the third tracer of Halo=10 in a BH parent is (python indexing convention pseudocode):</p>

<div class="collapse in lang_python"><div class="highlight"><pre><span></span><span class="nv">start</span> <span class="o">=</span> meta.hdf5/Halo/TracerOffset/bhs<span class="o">[</span><span class="m">10</span><span class="o">]</span>
<span class="nv">temp_K</span> <span class="o">=</span> temp.hdf5/temp<span class="o">[</span>start + <span class="m">2</span>, :<span class="o">]</span>
plot<span class="o">(</span> temp.hdf5/redshifts<span class="o">[</span>:<span class="o">]</span>, temp_K <span class="o">)</span>
</pre></div>
</div>

<p>while the evolution of distance from the halo center, in units of the virial radius, of all tracers in Subhalo=20 in star parents is given by: 

<div class="collapse in lang_python"><div class="highlight"><pre><span></span><span class="nv">start</span> <span class="o">=</span> meta.hdf5/Subhalo/TracerOffset/stars<span class="o">[</span><span class="m">20</span><span class="o">]</span>
<span class="nv">length</span> <span class="o">=</span> meta.hdf5/Subhalo/TracerLength/stars<span class="o">[</span><span class="m">20</span><span class="o">]</span>
<span class="nv">rad_rvir</span> <span class="o">=</span> rad_rvir.hdf5/rad_rvir<span class="o">[</span>start:start+length, :<span class="o">]</span>
<span class="k">for</span> i in range<span class="o">(</span>length<span class="o">)</span>:
  plot<span class="o">(</span> rad_rvir.hdf5/redshifts<span class="o">[</span>:<span class="o">]</span>, rad_rvir<span class="o">[</span>i,:<span class="o">]</span> <span class="o">)</span>
</pre></div>
</div>

<p>If you want all tracers in a halo/subhalo regardless of their z=0 parent type, then you must extract and combine the 
   three types. If you want a subset of tracers of a given halo/subhalo at z=0 (e.g. only those in some radial shell), 
   you must intersect the set of these IDs with the subset of meta.hdf5/ParentIDs for that halo/subhalo (or equivalently, 
   with the entirety of meta.hdf5/ParentIDs, the only penalty being speed) and use the resulting indices.</p>

 <p>For complete definitions of each value, see the following table and 
  <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2005.09654">Nelson+ (2019b)</a>
  where they were first used and presented. Citation to this paper is requested if you use these data catalogs. 
  For more information and examples related to the Monte Carlo tracer particles, see <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1305.2195">Genel+ (2013)</a>, 
  <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1301.6753">Nelson+ (2013)</a>, and <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1410.5425">Nelson+ (2015)</a>.</p>

<p></p>

<p>Simulation and snapshot coverage:
  <ul>
    <li>Currently available for the 3 resolution levels of TNG100: 
      <a href="/api/TNG100-1/files/tracer_tracks/">TNG100-1</a>,
      <a href="/api/TNG100-2/files/tracer_tracks/">TNG100-2</a>,
      <a href="/api/TNG100-3/files/tracer_tracks/">TNG100-3</a>.
    <li>All tracers in all parent types (gas cells, stars, and black holes), in all FoF halos at $z=0$.</li>
    <li>For each, time tracks have twenty entries, corresponding to the twenty "full" snapshots in TNG100.</li>
  </ul>
</p>

<p>The contents of the 'meta' file are:</p>

<div style="text-align:center;">
  <table class="table table-bordered table-condensed eoTable">
    <tr>
    <th style='width:15%'> Dataset Name </th>
    <th style='width:10%'> Shape </th>
    <th style='width:8%'> Type </th>
    <th> Description </th>
    </tr>

    <tr><td> /TracerIDs
    </td><td> N_tr
    </td><td> uint64
    </td><td> The ordered list of tracer IDs in this catalog. Corresponds to all tracers in all FoF halos at z=0. The same 'group ordered' scheme of the snapshots is followed, i.e. the tracers which belong to successive FoF halos are sequential, and likewise for successive Subfind subhalos, modulo FoF fuzz. 
    </td></tr>

    <tr><td> /ParentIDs
    </td><td> N_tr
    </td><td> uint64
    </td><td> The ordered list of the IDs of the parents of each tracer in this catalog. 
    </td></tr>

    <tr><td> /Halo/TracerLength/{gas,stars,bhs}
    </td><td> N_groups
    </td><td> int32
    </td><td> A dataset for each of <code>{gas,stars,bhs}</code> which gives the total number (length) of tracers in each FoF halo, at z=0, with parents of <i>that</i> type. 
    </td></tr>

    <tr><td> /Halo/TracerOffset/{gas,stars,bhs}
    </td><td> N_groups
    </td><td> int64
    </td><td>   A dataset for each of <code>{gas,stars,bhs}</code> which gives the offset (i.e. cumulative sum of preceeding lengths) of tracers for each FoF halo, at z=0, with parents of that type. For a given parent type, the set of tracer indices in halo ID <code>N</code> at z=0 is then given by <code>[TracerOffset[N], TracerOffset[N]+1, ..., TracerOffset[N]+TracerLength[N]-1]</code>. The corresponding tracer IDs are given by <code>TracerIDs[TracerOffset[N]:TracerOffset+TracerLength[N]-1]</code>, the indices inclusive. 
    </td></tr>

    <tr><td> /Subhalo/TracerLength/{gas,stars,bhs}
    </td><td> N_subhalos
    </td><td> int32
    </td><td> Same as TracerLength above, but for subhalos.
    </td></tr>

    <tr><td> /Subhalo/TracerOffset/{gas,stars,bhs}
    </td><td> N_subhalos
    </td><td> int64
    </td><td> Same as TracerOffset above, but for subhalos.
    </td></tr>

  </table>
</div>

<p>For the data files, all values which are computed with respect to evolving halo properties use the Sublink tree. 
   Tracers are associated with the central subhalo of the FoF halo they belong to at z=0, and the main progenitor 
   branch (MPB) of that subhalo is used backwards in time. If the MPB is untracked at a snapshot, these values 
   (rad_rvir,vrad,angmom) are set to NaN at that snapshot. Values with respect to the subhalo center use SubhaloPos 
   (maximally bound particle position), while those with respect to the subhalo velocity uses the mass-weighted mean 
   velocity of all star particles in the subhalo (if the subhalo has no stars, these values are then recorded as NaN).</p>

<p>The structure of the data files is: </p>

<div style="text-align:center;">
  <table class="table table-bordered table-condensed eoTable">
    <tr>
    <th style='width:10%'> File </th>
    <th style='width:15%'> Dataset Name </th>
    <th style='width:12%'> Shape </th>
    <th style='width:8%'> Type </th>
    <th style='width:10%'> Units </th>
    <th> Description </th>
    </tr>

    <tr><td> (all)
    </td><td> /snaps
    </td><td> N_snaps
    </td><td> int32
    </td><td> --
    </td><td> The snapshot numbers corresponding to each of the recorded times (the first dimension of the multi-dimensional data array). Exists in and the same for all data files. 
    </td></tr>

    <tr><td> (all)
    </td><td> /redshifts
    </td><td> N_snaps
    </td><td> float32
    </td><td> --
    </td><td> The redshifts corresponding to each of the recorded times (the second dimension of each multi-dimensional data array). Exists in and the same for all data files.
    </td></tr>

    <tr><td> <code>*_temp.hdf5</code>
    </td><td> /temp
    </td><td> (N_tr, N_snaps)
    </td><td> float32
    </td><td> $\rm{Kelvin}$
    </td><td> The temperature of the parent gas cell for each tracer at each time. Tracers in non-gas parents record NaN at that snapshot. (May be in log10).
    </td></tr>

    <tr><td> <code>*_sfr.hdf5</code>
    </td><td> /sfr
    </td><td> (N_tr, N_snaps)
    </td><td> float32
    </td><td> $M_\odot / \rm{yr}$
    </td><td> The star formation rate of the parent gas cell for each tracer at each time. Tracers in non-gas parents record NaN at that snapshot. (May be in log10).
    </td></tr>

    <tr><td> <code>*_entr.hdf5</code>
    </td><td> /entr
    </td><td> (N_tr, N_snaps)
    </td><td> float32
    </td><td> $\rm{K} \,\rm{cm}^2$
    </td><td> The entropy of the parent gas cell for each tracer at each time, calculated as $\rm{P} / \rho^{\gamma}$. Tracers in non-gas parents record NaN at that snapshot. (May be in log10).
    </td></tr>

    <!--
    <tr><td> <code>*_metal.hdf5</code>
    </td><td> /metal
    </td><td> (N_tr, N_snaps)
    </td><td> float32
    </td><td> -- (not solar)
    </td><td> The metallicity of the parent gas cell or star, for each tracer at each time (i.e. the <code>GFM_Metallicity</code> field). Tracers in BH parents record NaN at that snapshot.
    </td></tr>
    -->

    <tr><td> <code>*_subhalo_id.hdf5</code>
    </td><td> /subhalo_id
    </td><td> (N_tr, N_snaps)
    </td><td> int32
    </td><td> --
    </td><td> The subhalo ID to which the tracer belongs at that snapshot.
    </td></tr>

    <tr><td> <code>*_parent_indextype.hdf5</code>
    </td><td> /parent_indextype
    </td><td> (N_tr, N_snaps)
    </td><td> int64
    </td><td> --
    </td><td> Encodes the snapshot index of the parent (by type) as well as the parent type. The value equals <code>(type*1e11 + index)</code>. Therefore, for example, if a tracer is in a BH parent at a given snapshot, parent_indextype will have a value between 5e11 and 6e11, and its value minus 5e11 will provide the global index of any PartType5 dataset in the snapshot corresponding to its parent.
    </td></tr>

    <tr><td> <code>*_rad_rvir.hdf5</code>
    </td><td> /rad_rvir
    </td><td> (N_tr, N_snaps)
    </td><td> float32
    </td><td>
    </td><td> Radial distance of the parent from the subhalo center, of the MPB at this snapshot, normalized by the virial radius $r_{\rm 200,crit}$ of the progenitor halo at that snapshot. 
    </td></tr>

    <tr><td> <code>*_pos.hdf5</code>
    </td><td> /pos
    </td><td> (N_tr, 3, N_snaps)
    </td><td> float32
    </td><td> $\rm{ckpc/h}$
    </td><td> The x,y,z coordinates for each tracer at each time (comoving code units), of the parent gas cell, star, or BH.
    </td></tr>

    <tr><td> <code>*_vel.hdf5</code>
    </td><td> /vel
    </td><td> (N_tr, 3, N_snaps)
    </td><td> float32
    </td><td> $\rm{km}\sqrt{a}/s$
    </td><td> The x,y,z velocity for each tracer at each time (comoving code units), of the parent gas cell, star, or BH.
    </td></tr>

    <tr><td> <code>*_vrad.hdf5</code>
    </td><td> /vrad
    </td><td> (N_tr, N_snaps)
    </td><td> float32
    </td><td> $\rm{km/s}$
    </td><td> Radial velocity of the parent with respect to the subhalo center and velocity, of the MPB at this snapshot, with the Hubble expansion added in. Negative denotes inwards.
    </td></tr>

    <tr><td> <code>*_angmom.hdf5</code>
    </td><td> /angmom
    </td><td> (N_tr, N_snaps)
    </td><td> float32
    </td><td> $\rm{kpc \,km/s}$
    </td><td> Magnitude of the specific angular momentum vector of the parent, with respect to the subhalo center and velocity, of the MPB at this snapshot, accounting for Hubble expansion.
    </td></tr>

  </table>
</div>

<p><b>Important note! These files are very large.</b> For TNG100, the one-dimensional data files (e.g. temp, entr, vrad) 
  are ~300 GB each. Therefore, functionality has been added to the data access API to enable the retrieval of tracer 
  tracks on a per-halo or per-subhalo basis. In particular, an API request for a <a href="/data/docs/api/#cutout_query">halo/subhalo cutout</a>, of particles/cells from the snapshot can specity <code>tracer_tracks</code> as the 'particle type', together with any of the dataset names above (namely, <code>TracerIDs</code>, <code>ParentIDs</code>, <code>temp</code>, <code>entr</code>, and so on). For example, whereas a normal cutout request for the positions of all gas cells of halo ID 1000 in TNG100-1 at redshift zero would be:</p>

<pre>
https://www.tng-project.org/api/TNG100-1/snapshots/99/halos/1000/cutout.hdf5?gas=Coordinates
</pre>

<p>A cutout request for the temperature tracks of all the tracers belonging to that halo would be:

<pre>
https://www.tng-project.org/api/TNG100-1/snapshots/99/halos/1000/cutout.hdf5?tracer_tracks=temp
</pre>



      <p></p>
      
      

      <a name="sec5b" class="anchor"></a>
      <hr class="docs">
      <h3 class="docs">(b) Star Formation Rates</h3>
      
      
<p>A catalog of time-averaged, rather than instantaneous, star formation rates of galaxies.
To do so the SFRs are derived the stellar particles actually produced across different time spans, 
using their initial mass at birth. This is in contrast to all values in the group/subhalo catalogs, 
which are instantaneous values (measured from the gas cells). The time-averaged methodology, across
a given period of Myr or Gyr, better reflects the values obtained via various observational tracers.
For complete definitions on the calculation of each value, see the following table,  
<a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1812.07584">Donnari+ (2019)</a>, and
<a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1902.05553">Pillepich+ (2019)</a>
where they were first presented. Citation to these two papers is requested if you use these data catalogs.</p>

<p></p>

<p>Simulation and snapshot coverage:
  <ul>
    <li>Available for all baryonic simulations: 
      <a href="/api/TNG50-1/files/star_formation_rates.hdf5">TNG50-1</a>,
      <a href="/api/TNG50-2/files/star_formation_rates.hdf5">TNG50-2</a>,
      <a href="/api/TNG50-3/files/star_formation_rates.hdf5">TNG50-3</a>,
      <a href="/api/TNG50-4/files/star_formation_rates.hdf5">TNG50-4</a>,
      <a href="/api/TNG100-1/files/star_formation_rates.hdf5">TNG100-1</a>,
      <a href="/api/TNG100-2/files/star_formation_rates.hdf5">TNG100-2</a>,
      <a href="/api/TNG100-3/files/star_formation_rates.hdf5">TNG100-3</a>,
      <a href="/api/TNG300-1/files/star_formation_rates.hdf5">TNG300-1</a>,
      <a href="/api/TNG300-2/files/star_formation_rates.hdf5">TNG300-2</a>,
      <a href="/api/TNG300-3/files/star_formation_rates.hdf5">TNG300-3</a>,
      <a href="/api/Illustris-1/files/star_formation_rates.hdf5">Illustris-1</a>,
      <a href="/api/Illustris-2/files/star_formation_rates.hdf5">Illustris-2</a>,
      <a href="/api/Illustris-3/files/star_formation_rates.hdf5">Illustris-3</a>.</li>
    <li>All snapshots/redshifts.</li>
    <li>Restricted to subhalos with a reasonable number of stellar particles (>~ 100).</li>
  </ul>
</p>


<div style="text-align:center;">
  <table class="table table-bordered table-condensed eoTable">
    <tr>
    <th style='width:30%'> Group Name </th>
    <th> Units </th>
    <th> Description </th>
    </tr>
    <tr><td> /Snapshot_N/SubfindID
    </td><td> -
    </td><td> The <a href="/data/docs/faq/#gen3">Subfind IDs</a> these values correspond to at this snapshot.
    </tr>
    <tr><td> /Snapshot_N/SFR_MsunPerYrs_in_r5pkpc_{X}Myrs
    </td><td> Msun/yr
    </td><td> The galaxy SFR including stars within a 3D aperture of 5 physical kpc, averaged 
      across the last <code>{X}</code> Myr. <br>This roughly mimics the SDSS fiber aperture.
    </td></tr>
    <tr><td> /Snapshot_N/SFR_MsunPerYrs_in_InRad_{X}Myrs
    </td><td> Msun/yr
    </td><td> The galaxy SFR including stars within a 3D aperture of two times the stellar 
      half mass radius, <br>averaged across the last <code>{X}</code> Myr.
    </td></tr>
    <tr><td> /Snapshot_N/SFR_MsunPerYrs_in_r30pkpc_{X}Myrs
    </td><td> Msun/yr
    </td><td> The galaxy SFR including stars within a 3D aperture of 30 physical kpc, 
      averaged across the last <code>{X}</code> Myr.
    </td></tr>
    <tr><td> /Snapshot_N/SFR_MsunPerYrs_in_all_{X}Myrs
    </td><td> Msun/yr
    </td><td> The galaxy SFR including all gravitationally bound stars of the subhalo,
      averaged across the last <code>{X}</code> Myr.
    </td></tr>
  </table>
</div>

<p>For all measurements, time intervals <code>{X}</code> exist for <code>X = 10, 50, 100, 200, 1000</code> (Myr).</p>


      <p></p>



      <a name="sec5c" class="anchor"></a>
      <hr class="docs">
      <h3 class="docs">(c) Stellar Circularities, Angular Momenta, Axis Ratios</h3>
      
      
<p>A catalog of circularities, angular momenta and axis ratios of the stellar component of galaxies. 
For complete definitions on the calculation of 
each value, see the following table and <a href="http://adsabs.harvard.edu/abs/2015arXiv150301117G">Genel+ (2015)</a>, 
where they were first presented. Citation to that paper is requested if you use these data catalogs.</p>

<p>The first four quantities are calculated after alignment with the angular momentum vector of the stars within 
10 times the stellar half-mass radius, and measure the quantities inside that radius. The <code>Circ*</code> fields are 
based on the distribution of the circularity parameter $\epsilon$. First the system is rotated such that the 
z-axis is aligned with the angular momentum vector as described above. Then, for every stellar particle with 
specific angular momentum $J_z$ we calculate $ \epsilon = \frac{ J_z }{ J(E) } $ where $J(E)$ is the maximum 
angular momentum of the stellar particles at positions between 50 before and 50 after the particle in question 
in a list where the stellar particles are sorted by their binding energy ($=U_{\rm grav} + v^2$).</p>

<p>Simulation and snapshot coverage:
  <ul>
    <li>Available for all baryonic simulations: 
      <a href="/api/TNG50-1/files/stellar_circs.hdf5">TNG50-1</a>,
      <a href="/api/TNG50-2/files/stellar_circs.hdf5">TNG50-2</a>,
      <a href="/api/TNG50-3/files/stellar_circs.hdf5">TNG50-3</a>,
      <a href="/api/TNG50-4/files/stellar_circs.hdf5">TNG50-4</a>,
      <a href="/api/TNG100-1/files/stellar_circs.hdf5">TNG100-1</a>,
      <a href="/api/TNG100-2/files/stellar_circs.hdf5">TNG100-2</a>,
      <a href="/api/TNG100-3/files/stellar_circs.hdf5">TNG100-3</a>,
      <a href="/api/TNG300-1/files/stellar_circs.hdf5">TNG300-1</a>,
      <a href="/api/TNG300-2/files/stellar_circs.hdf5">TNG300-2</a>,
      <a href="/api/TNG300-3/files/stellar_circs.hdf5">TNG300-3</a>,
      <a href="/api/Illustris-1/files/stellar_circs.hdf5">Illustris-1</a>
       (all snapshots/redshifts).</li>
    <li>Restricted to subhalos with stellar mass $M^\star > 3.4 \times 10^8 M_\odot$ (TNG50/TNG100/Illustris) or $M^\star > 10^{10} M_\odot$ (TNG300), 
        measured within the usual twice stellar half mass radius, and at least 100 stars.</li>
  </ul>
</p>


<div style="text-align:center;">
  <table class="table table-bordered table-condensed eoTable">
    <tr>
    <th> Group Name </th>
    <th> Units </th>
    <th> Description </th>
    </tr>
    <tr>
    <td> /Snapshot_N/SubfindID
    </td><td> -
    </td><td> The <a href="/data/docs/faq/#gen3">Subfind IDs</a> these values correspond to at this snapshot.
    </tr><tr>
    </td><td> /Snapshot_N/SpecificAngMom
    </td><td> $ km/s \times kpc $
    </td><td> The specific angular momentum of the stars.
    </tr><tr>
    </td><td> /Snapshot_N/CircAbove07Frac
    </td><td> -
    </td><td> The fractional mass of stars with $ \epsilon \gt 0.7 $. This is a common definition of the "disk" stars - those with significant (positive) rotational support.
    </tr><tr>
    </td><td> /Snapshot_N/CircAbove07MinusBelowNeg07Frac
    </td><td> -
    </td><td> The fractional mass of stars with $ \epsilon \gt 0.7 $ minus the fraction of stars with $ \epsilon \lt -0.7 $. This removes the contribution of the "bulge" to the "disk", assuming the bulge is symmetric around $ \epsilon=0 $.
    </tr><tr>
    </td><td> /Snapshot_N/CircTwiceBelow0Frac
    </td><td> -
    </td><td>  The fractional mass of stars with $ \epsilon \lt 0 $, multiplied by two. This is another common way in the literature to define the "bulge".
    </tr><tr>
    </td><td> /Snapshot_N/MassTensorEigenVals
    </td><td> $ kpc $
    </td><td> Three numbers for each galaxy that correspond to the eigenvalues of the mass tensor of the stellar mass inside the stellar $2R_{1/2}$. In a coordinate system that is aligned with the eigenvectors (principal axes), the component $i$ equals $M_i\equiv\sqrt{\sum\limits_j m_jr_{j,i}^2}/\sqrt{\sum\limits_j m_j}$, where $j$ enumerates over stellar particles inside that radius, $r_{j,i}$ is the distance of stellar particle $j$ in the $i$ axis from the most bound particle of the galaxy, and $m_j$ is its mass, and $i\in(1,2,3)$. They are sorted such that $M_1 \lt M_2 \lt M_3$. Example use: $M_1/\sqrt{M_2M_3}$ can represent the flatness of the galaxy.
    </tr><tr>
    </td><td> /Snapshot_N/ReducedMassTensorEigenVals
    </td><td> -
    </td><td> Similar to the above, except less weight is given to further away particles. The orientation of the system is the same, but the quantity measured for each axis is instead $M_i\equiv\sqrt{\sum\limits_j m_jr_{j,i}^2/R_j^2}/\sqrt{\sum\limits_j m_j}$, where $R_j\equiv\sqrt{\sum\limits_i r_{j,i}^2}$ is the distance of star $j$ from the centre of the galaxy.
    </td>
    </tr>
  </table>
</div>

<p>Note: For original Illustris (only), the "SpecificAngMom" and "Circ*" fields are available for snapshots 13 through 135, while
   the "*MassTensor*" fields are available for snapshots 38 through 135.</p>
<p>Note: In addition to the values measured within $10 R_E$, the "SpecificAngMom" and "Circ*" fields
   are also computed including all stars in the subhalo. These are available as the <code>_allstars</code> datasets.</p>



      <p></p>



      <a name="sec5d" class="anchor"></a>
      <hr class="docs">
      <h3 class="docs">(d) Subhalo Matching Between Runs</h3>

      <p>The possibility exists to cross-match subhalos between:

      <ul>
        <li>(i) matched baryonic and dark matter only runs (e.g. TNG100-1 and TNG100-1-Dark) runs, at the same resolution.</li>
        <li>(ii) two baryonic realizations of the same box at different resolutions (e.g. TNG100-1 and TNG100-2)</li>
        <li>(iii) two simulations of the same box with different physical models (e.g. TNG100-1 and Illustris-1)</li>
      </ul>

      <p>All baryonic runs have complete matching catalogs to their dark matter only counterparts, the first option <b>(i)</b> above.</p>
      <p>These are data products from: 
        <a href="http://arxiv.org/abs/1502.01339">Rodriguez-Gomez+ (2015; for SubLink)</a>, and 
        <a href="http://arxiv.org/abs/1504.00362">Nelson+ (2015; for LHaloTree)</a>.
      Citation to these papers is requested if you use this data.</p>

      <p>Simulation and snapshot coverage:
        <ul>
          <li>All baryonic runs at all resolutions, all snapshots, all subhalos:</li>
          <li><a href="/api/TNG50-1/files/subhalo_matching_to_dark.hdf5">TNG50-1</a>, 
              <a href="/api/TNG50-2/files/subhalo_matching_to_dark.hdf5">TNG50-2</a>, 
              <a href="/api/TNG50-3/files/subhalo_matching_to_dark.hdf5">TNG50-3</a>, 
              <a href="/api/TNG50-4/files/subhalo_matching_to_dark.hdf5">TNG50-4</a>.</li>
          <li><a href="/api/TNG100-1/files/subhalo_matching_to_dark.hdf5">TNG100-1</a>, 
              <a href="/api/TNG100-2/files/subhalo_matching_to_dark.hdf5">TNG100-2</a>,
              <a href="/api/TNG100-3/files/subhalo_matching_to_dark.hdf5">TNG100-3</a>.</li>
          <li><a href="/api/TNG300-1/files/subhalo_matching_to_dark.hdf5">TNG300-1</a>,
              <a href="/api/TNG300-2/files/subhalo_matching_to_dark.hdf5">TNG300-2</a>,
              <a href="/api/TNG300-3/files/subhalo_matching_to_dark.hdf5">TNG300-3</a>.</li>
        </ul>
      </p>

      <p>Data format: a single file <code>subhalo_matching_to_dark.hdf5</code> for each simulation. 
      Each contains one hundred groups named <code>Snapshot_N</code>.
      Within each group are two arrays, each with the same size, equal to the number of subhalos at the corresponding snapshot.
      The two arrays provide the results of two different matching algorithms, and should for the most part provide the same answer.
      Each array gives integer indices, whose value is the corresponding index of the matched subhalo in the DM only run. 
      If no suitable match exists, a value of <code>-1</code> is present for that subhalo index.</p>

      <ul>
        <li>The first array <code>SubhaloIndexDark_LHaloTree</code> is based on the LHaloTree matching algorithm. The matching is bidirectional, 
            i.e. TNG &lt;-&gt; DMO. In each case, the best subhalo candidate is chosen as that with the largest number of matching DM particles ($\alpha=0$).
            Only if the candidate in each direction agrees (bijective), then these matches are saved.</li>

        <li>The second array <code>SubhaloIndexDark_SubLink</code> is based on the SubLink weighting algorithm. The direction of the matching is
            TNG -&gt; DMO, i.e. for each subhalo in the baryonic physics box a best match is found in the DMO run.</li>
      </ul>

      <p style='margin-top: 2em;'>The second two types <b>(ii)</b> and <b>(iii)</b> of matching catalogs (between different resolution levels, 
      and between TNG100 and original Illustris), are available for selected runs and snapshots. Here the approach is 'Lagrangian matching', meaning 
      that the Lagrangian volumes of subhalos are identified and matched in the initial conditions. The method is described in 
      <a href="http://arxiv.org/abs/1801.10170">Lovell+ (2018)</a> to which citation is requested if you use these datasets.

       <p>Simulation and snapshot coverage:
        <ul>
          <li><a href="/api/TNG100-1/files/subhalo_matching_lagrangian/">TNG100-1</a> is matched to: TNG100-2, TNG100-3, and Illustris-1.</li>
          <li><a href="/api/TNG300-1/files/subhalo_matching_lagrangian/">TNG300-1</a> is matched to: TNG300-2 and TNG300-3.</li>
          <li>For each, redshifts <code>z=0</code>, <code>z=0.5</code>, <code>z=1</code>, and <code>z=2</code> only! All subhalos.</li>
        </ul>
      </p>

      <p>Data format: each file <code>LagrangeMatches_run1_run2_NNN.hdf5</code> provides matching from <b>run1</b> to <b>run2</b> at a given snapshot.
      The following datasets are present:</p>

      <ul>
        <li><code>SubhaloIndexFrom</code> - for each matched subhalo, the subhalo index in Run1.</li>
        <li><code>SubhaloFinalSnapTo</code> - for each matched subhalo, final snapshot at which subhalo exists in target Run2.</li>
        <li><code>SubhaloIndexTo</code> - for each matched subhalo, subhalo index in target Run2 at SubhaloFinalSnapTo snapshot.</li>
        <li><code>Score</code> - a value between 0 and 1 denoting the matching quality: 1 signifies the best quality match. &lt;0.5 signifies match likely not achieved.</li>
      </ul>


      <a name="sec5e" class="anchor"></a>
      <hr class="docs">
      <h3 class="docs">(e) Stellar Projected Sizes</h3>

      
<p>A catalog of stellar "sizes", mostly half-light radii, all in 2D projection. For more details of the 
modeling see <a href="https://ui.adsabs.harvard.edu/abs/2018MNRAS.474.3976G/abstract">Genel+ (2018)</a>, 
where they were first presented. Citation to that paper is requested if you use these data catalogs.</p>

<p>Simulation and snapshot coverage:
  <ul>
    <li>Available for all baryonic simulations: 
      <a href="/api/TNG50-1/files/stellar_sizes/">TNG50-1</a>,
      <a href="/api/TNG100-1/files/stellar_sizes/">TNG100-1</a> and 
      <a href="/api/TNG300-1/files/stellar_sizes/">TNG300-1</a>.
    </li>
    <li>Eight snapshots: z=0, 0.5, 1.0, 1.5, 2.0, 3.0, 4.0, 5.0.</li>
    <li>Restricted to subhalos with stellar mass $M^\star > 3 \times 10^8 M_\odot$ 
        measured within the usual twice stellar half mass radius, and at least 100 stars.</li>
  </ul>
</p>

<p>There is one file for every snapshot, containing a single dataset <code>/Rhalf/</code>, which is a 
<code>10 x 5 x Nsubs</code> shaped array. Namely, for each subhalo there are 10x5 different values of 
half-<i>something</i> radii, as follows:

<ul>
  <li>Along the dimension of length 10, the values correspond to different somethings, in order, as follows: 
  half-mass, half-star-formation-rate, half-light in the 8 bands of the “stellar photometrics”, namely 
  U, B, V, K, g, r, i, z. Note that the impact of dust attenuation/scattering is not taken into account.</li>
  <li>Along the dimension of length 5, the values correspond to projections along various lines of sight, 
  in order, as follows: face-on, largest edge-on, smallest edge-on, random edge-on, box z-axis, where:</li>
  <ul>
  <li>The face-on direction is set according to the moment of inertia tensor of the star-formation 
  distribution within $2 R_{1/2,\star}$ (unless there are fewer than 50 star-forming cells, in 
  which case it is according to the moment of inertia tensor of the stellar mass distribution within 
  $R_{1/2,\star}$.</li>
  <li>All three edge-on directions are from lines of sights that are perpendicular to the face-on direction. 
  The largest and smallest edge-on directions mean the particular such lines of sight that produce the 
  largest/smallest, respectively, projected radii for the star-formation distribution within 
  $2 R_{1/2,\star}$. The random edge-on direction is indeed at a random line of sight within 
  the "edge-on plane".</li>
  <li>For a "random" angle, choose the projection along the box z-axis, which is of course "random" with 
  respect to each and every individual galaxy, since there is no preferred direction to the simulation.</li>
  </ul>
</ul>
</p>

<p>All units are physical kpc.</p>

<p>Note: a value of <code>-1</code> indicates that the subhalo falls outside of the selection described above, 
and no data is available for this object.</p>



      <p></p>

 

      <a name="sec5f" class="anchor"></a>
      <hr class="docs">
      <h3 class="docs">(f) Blackhole Mergers and Details</h3>

      
<p>This catalog is comprised of two data sets: 'mergers' and 'details'. The mergers file contains
a record of each BH-BH merger in the simulation. The details file contain properties of each BH 
at significantly higher time resolution than the snapshots. In both cases the information is 
extracted from output files separate from the snapshots, and so represents additional data which 
is <i>otherwise not available from the snapshots alone.</i> Here we distinguish the two BHs which 
participate in the merger as 'in' and 'out' BH. The difference, which during the simulation is chosen 
randomly by the code, is which BH ID number persists along with the remnant after the merger. The 
'out' BH survives after the merger, increased in mass by that of the 'in' BH--which no longer 
exists after the merger event.</p>

<p>There exists one 
<code>blackhole_mergers.hdf5</code> and one <code>blackhole_details.hdf5</code> per baryonic run. 
All datasets corresponding to physical values are in code units, with 
masses in units of $10^{10} M_\odot / h$, 
mass accretion rates in units of $10.22 M_\odot / \rm{yr}$, 
gas densities in units of $(10^{10} M_\odot / h) / (\rm{ckpc} / h)^3$, 
and gas sound speeds in units of $\rm{km / s}$.
They were generated with the 
<a href="https://www.github.com/illustristng/illustris_blackholes">illustris_blackholes</a> code. 
Citation recommmended to <a href="http://arxiv.org/abs/1606.01900">Kelley et al. (2016)</a> and 
<a href="http://arxiv.org/abs/1508.01524">Blecha et al. (2016)</a> as appropriate.</p>

<p>Simulation and snapshot coverage:
  <ul>
    <li>Available for all TNG baryonic runs, as well as all original Illustris baryonic runs.</li>
    <li>Black hole mergers: 
        <a href="/api/TNG50-1/files/blackhole_mergers.hdf5">TNG50-1</a>,
        <a href="/api/TNG50-2/files/blackhole_mergers.hdf5">TNG50-2</a>,
        <a href="/api/TNG50-3/files/blackhole_mergers.hdf5">TNG50-3</a>,
        <a href="/api/TNG50-4/files/blackhole_mergers.hdf5">TNG50-4</a>,
        <a href="/api/TNG100-1/files/blackhole_mergers.hdf5">TNG100-1</a>,
        <a href="/api/TNG100-2/files/blackhole_mergers.hdf5">TNG100-2</a>,
        <a href="/api/TNG100-3/files/blackhole_mergers.hdf5">TNG100-3</a>,
        <a href="/api/TNG300-1/files/blackhole_mergers.hdf5">TNG300-1</a>,
        <a href="/api/TNG300-2/files/blackhole_mergers.hdf5">TNG300-2</a>,
        <a href="/api/TNG300-3/files/blackhole_mergers.hdf5">TNG300-3</a>.
    </li>
    <li>Black hole details:
        <a href="/api/TNG50-1/files/blackhole_details.hdf5">TNG50-1</a>,
        <a href="/api/TNG50-2/files/blackhole_details.hdf5">TNG50-2</a>,
        <a href="/api/TNG50-3/files/blackhole_details.hdf5">TNG50-3</a>,
        <a href="/api/TNG50-4/files/blackhole_details.hdf5">TNG50-4</a>,
        <a href="/api/TNG100-1/files/blackhole_details.hdf5">TNG100-1</a>,
        <a href="/api/TNG100-2/files/blackhole_details.hdf5">TNG100-2</a>,
        <a href="/api/TNG100-3/files/blackhole_details.hdf5">TNG100-3</a>,
        <a href="/api/TNG300-1/files/blackhole_details.hdf5">TNG300-1</a>,
        <a href="/api/TNG300-2/files/blackhole_details.hdf5">TNG300-2</a>,
        <a href="/api/TNG300-3/files/blackhole_details.hdf5">TNG300-3</a>.
    </li>
    <li><b>IMPORTANT NOTE:</b> For both the mergers and details files, there are a few small <i>missing data gaps</i>. <b>In general these files are fragile and may contain inconsistencies.</b>
      In TNG100-1, this includes redshift periods [0.96 - 0.98], [1.18 - 1.20], [1.27 - 1.40], [2.06 - 2.48]. 
      For Illustris-1, data is missing in the redshift range from approximately z = 0.14 to z = 0.38, where roughly 
      half of the BH data is missing, except at the higher-z end of the range, where nearly all of the mergers are 
      missing around snapshots 110-111. All other runs are unaffected. 
    This information was lost to data corruption and cannot be recovered, and appropriate care should be taken in analysis of this data.</li>
  </ul>
</p>

<p>Blackhole mergers:</p>

<div style="text-align:center;">
  <table class="table table-bordered table-condensed eoTable">
    <tr>
    <th> Dataset </th>
    <th> Shape/Datatype </th>
    <th> Description </th>
    </tr>
    <tr>
    <td> /Header/unique_ids
    </td><td> (NumBHsTot) uint64
    </td><td> The ID numbers of all unique BH participating in mergers.
    </td></tr><tr>

    </td><td> (attribute)
    </td><td> The total number of mergers stored $(N)$.

    </td></tr><tr>
    <td> /tree/*
    </td><td> -
    </td><td> Information describing the BH merger tree.  If one of the below events does not 
              exist, the value in the array is -1, NOTE: not zero. For example, if 
              <code>next[123] = 345</code>, then merger 345 is the next merger that  
              the BH remnant from merger 123 is involved in. If, <code>prev_in[345] == 123</code> 
              and <code>prev_out[345] = -1</code>, then the 123 merger remnant is the 'in' BH of 
              merger 345, and the other BH of that merger was never in a previous merger (and so has 
              a <code>prev</code> value of -1).  
    </td></tr><tr>

              searched by trying to match the 'in' BH just before merger, and the 'out' BH both

    </td></tr><tr>

      the Eddington limit or local pressure criterion are applied, and thus will not in general reflect 
      the true mdot of the BH used during the simulation. Should be used with caution.
    </td></tr><tr>
    <td> /details/rho
    </td><td> (N,3) float
    </td><td> Local gas density in the vicinity of the blackhole.
    </td></tr><tr>
    <td> /details/cs
    </td><td> (N,3) float
    </td><td> Local gas sound-speed in the vicinity of the blackhole.

    </td></tr><tr>

    </td></tr><tr>

    </td></tr><tr>

    </td></tr><tr>
    <td> /snapshot
    </td><td> (N) int
    </td><td> Output snapshot during which, or immediately following, this merger event occured.
    </td></tr>
  </table>
</div>
